<details>
<summary>**Tool result** <sub>toolu_01CZjTMna5Phskfma1Zbkerz</sub></summary>

````
# Parcel rates: USPS, UPS and FedEx list prices for a small online shop

A dashboard that prices a shop's own parcels at the published prices of the
three US parcel carriers, for every year from 2023 to 2026, and shows which
carrier is cheapest, where the breakeven points are, how much of the bill is
surcharges, and how the cost moved year by year.

## Run it

Requires Docker with Compose v2. No accounts, keys or database setup.

```sh
docker compose up --build
```

Then open **http://localhost:8088**. The first build takes a minute or two.
Click "Load sample mix" to load an illustrative example parcel set, or upload your
own CSV (columns below). Stop with `Ctrl+C`, then `docker compose down`.

Only port 8088 is published. nginx serves the built dashboard and proxies
`/api/` to the backend container, which is not reachable from the host. The
rate tables are baked into the backend image; the app never fetches anything
from the carriers at run time.

## What it looks like

Captured by the Playwright suite against `docker compose up` with the sample
mix and the default controls (2026 prices, USPS commercial, $9.99 charged per
parcel). The review of every capture, desktop and phone, light and dark, is in
[docs/ux-review.md](docs/ux-review.md).

**Q1. One parcel.** A 3 lb box going 600 miles to a home costs $8.95 with USPS
Ground Advantage, $1.04 under the $9.99 the shop charges; FedEx Home Delivery
and UPS Ground cost about $30, partly because they bill the box at its 6 lb
dimensional weight. Each line item cites the rate file it came from.

![Q1: the cheapest service for one parcel with its line items](docs/screenshots/q1-quote-desktop.png)

**Q2. Breakevens.** For home delivery USPS Ground Advantage is cheapest at every
weight up to 450 miles (zones 1-4); from 800 miles on it stops winning at 27 lb,
UPS Ground or FedEx Home Delivery take over up to 50 lb, and USPS wins again
from 51 lb.

![Q2: weight by distance map of the cheapest ground carrier](docs/screenshots/q2-breakeven-desktop.png)

**Q3. The whole mix.** Shipping all 307 sample parcels with USPS costs $10.98 a
parcel against $31.14 with UPS and $31.22 with FedEx, yet the shop still loses
$0.31 a parcel: 40 % of parcels (124 of 307) ship at a loss even with the
cheapest carrier.

![Q3: blended cost per carrier, the loss verdict and the cost split](docs/screenshots/q3-blended-desktop.png)

**Q4. Year over year.** The same mix with USPS went from $9.17 in 2023 to $10.98
in 2026 (+19.7 %), 3.3 points more than the +16.4 % USPS announced over those
years; at its latest announced +6.5 % it would cost $11.70 in 2027.

![Q4: cost per parcel by year against the announced increases](docs/screenshots/q4-yearly-desktop.png)

**On a phone** the answer is on the first screen and the assumptions sit
behind "Adjust" (left: Q1; right: Q3 in dark mode).

<p>
  <img src="docs/screenshots/q1-quote-mobile.png" alt="Q1 on a 375 px phone" width="300">
  <img src="docs/screenshots/q3-blended-mobile-dark.png" alt="Q3 on a 375 px phone, dark mode" width="300">
</p>

## The problem and the user

The user is the owner of a small online shop that sends a few hundred to a few
thousand parcels a month, has no negotiated carrier contract, and therefore
pays published prices. The carrier is usually chosen by habit, and the flat
shipping price charged to customers was set once and not revisited.

The dashboard supports two decisions: which carrier and service to use for
each kind of parcel, and whether the flat shipping price still covers the real
cost once surcharges and yearly price increases are included.

### What it answers

| # | Question | The view |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | For one parcel (weight, size, distance, home or business, speed), which service is cheapest once surcharges are included, and what are the line items? | The cheapest total for the chosen speed comes first, with its saving over the next cheapest and the margin against your price. Below it, every carrier's cheapest service ranked, each with base rate, every surcharge (with its source page) and fuel. The form can start from one of your loaded parcels. |
| 2 | Where are the breakeven points between the Postal Service and FedEx or UPS ground, by weight and distance? | A weight by distance heatmap coloured by the cheapest ground carrier, for home or business delivery, with a heavy line where USPS stops winning and a plain-words readout of the breakeven weights; every cell's prices on hover, tap or focus, and the grid as a table. |
| 3 | For the whole parcel set, what is the blended cost per parcel with each carrier, and how much of it is surcharges? | The cheapest single carrier's cost per parcel and the "best mix" (cheapest carrier per parcel), then a yes or no on whether your shipping price covers the cost, the margin per parcel and the share shipped at a loss per carrier. A stacked chart (or table) splits the cost into base, the largest surcharges and fuel. Chips narrow everything to one kind of parcel (weight, distance, home or business, speed). |
| 4 | How did that blended cost change year by year, compared with the average increase the carriers announced, and which parcels drove it? | The cheapest carrier's change since the first year against the compounded announced increases, a year by year chart (dollars or index) with the announced path, how much of the biggest rise was fuel, the segments that drove each step, and a next-year slider that projects the cost. |

Global controls: price year, USPS rate card, fuel surcharge override, peak
season toggle, and the flat price charged per parcel.

Parcel CSV columns: `weight_lb, length_in, width_in, height_in,
distance_miles, residential, speed` (`ground`, `two_three_day`, `next_day`),
plus optional `quantity`, `price_charged`, `delivery_area`,
`nonstandard_packaging`. The full schema is in
[docs/data/SCHEMA.md](docs/data/SCHEMA.md).

**The sample mix** is an illustrative home-goods shop, an example and not real
shipping data, generated by `backend/scripts/make_sample_parcels.py` with a
fixed seed: 200 rows, 307 parcels, mostly light ground parcels in boxes under
one cubic foot (about 80 % under 5 lb), about 7 % bulky boxes over one cubic
foot, 4 % two to three day and 1 % next day, about 80 % to homes, distances
weighted toward the middle zones, and a handful in delivery areas. Ground rows
leave `price_charged` empty and use the flat price control; expedited rows
carry what a shop would charge at checkout, $19.99 for two to three day and
$39.99 for next day, because real shops charge more for faster shipping. At the
default controls (2026, USPS commercial, $9.99) the mix opens near break-even:
USPS, the cheapest carrier, costs $10.98 per parcel against $10.67 charged on
average (−$0.31 per parcel), and the 40 % of parcels shipped at a loss are
mostly the bulky boxes, parcels over 5 lb and the far zones.

## Data provenance

All prices come from the carriers' own published rate documents:

| Carrier | Base rates | Surcharge rules | Years |
|---|---|---|---|
| USPS | Postal Explorer CSV zips per price change; Notice 123 PDFs | Notice 123 footnotes; Domestic Mail Manual for dimensional weight | 2023 (July tables, Ground Advantage launch) to 2026 |
| UPS | Daily Rates guide PDF per year | Surcharge section of the same guide; demand surcharge PDFs | 2023 to 2026 |
| FedEx | Standard List Rates XLSX (PDF for 2023 and 2024) | Yearly surcharge-change PDFs and Service Guides | 2023 to 2026, plus 2027 as a forward year |

**Collection.** Every raw file is listed in

"""Generate backend/app/sample_parcels.csv, the bundled ILLUSTRATIVE parcel mix.

This is an example, not real shipping data. It imitates a small online
home-goods shop (candles, mugs, linens, small decor): mostly light ground
parcels in small boxes, a minority of bulky boxes, and few expedited orders.

Target mix (issue #22):

- about 80 % of parcels weigh under 5 lb; every box except the bulky ones is
  under 1 cubic foot (1,728 cu in), so dimensional weight rarely applies;
- a fixed 12 rows of bulky boxes between 1 and 2 cubic feet (throws, lamps),
  about 7 % of parcels;
- a fixed 12 rows ship two to three day and 3 rows next day (about 4 % and
  1 % of parcels), always single light parcels; the rest ship ground;
- about 76 % of rows residential; distances spread over every zone, weighted
  toward the middle zones (3 to 6); five rows in delivery areas (four
  delivery area, one extended); two rows with non-standard packaging;
- 200 rows whose quantities sum to about 300 parcels.

Price charged. Ground parcels leave ``price_charged`` empty and use the flat
price control ($9.99 by default). Expedited parcels carry what a shop would
charge at checkout for faster shipping: $19.99 for two to three day and
$39.99 for next day. Small shops usually price an expedited option at two to
four times their standard rate, and these amounts sit near the published USPS
Priority Mail and Priority Mail Express prices for the light parcels people
choose to rush. Without them every expedited parcel would ship at $9.99 and
show a loss no real shop would accept.

Tuning target: the default view (2026, USPS commercial, default fuel, peak
off, flat $9.99) opens near break-even overall for the cheapest carrier (blended
margin within about $1 per parcel), with losses where they would be in reality.
Measured with the rate engine by ``--check`` (no engine or data change):

    200 rows, 307 parcels: 81 % under 5 lb, 7.2 % over 1 cu ft, 3.9 % two to
    three day, 1.0 % next day, 80 % residential, 2.3 % in delivery areas
    revenue $10.67 per parcel (flat $9.99 plus the expedited prices)
    USPS (cheapest): $10.98 per parcel, margin -$0.31 per parcel, 40 % of parcels at a loss
    UPS $31.14 and FedEx $31.22 per parcel (list prices, residential, fuel)
    best mix: all 307 parcels with USPS, same as USPS alone
    losses (USPS): bulky boxes -$10.69 per parcel (all at a loss), 5 to 20 lb
```

{"body":"The current sample (300 parcels) makes the shop look badly mispriced: 22% of parcels are bulky boxes over 1 cu ft and 13% ship expedited, all at the flat \\$9.99, so 71% ship at a loss even with USPS. That headline is an artefact of the sample, not an insight.\n\nDecision (owner, 2026-10-23):\n- Mostly light ground parcels, a minority of bulky boxes, far fewer expedited.\n- Use the optional per-parcel `price_charged` column: expedited parcels carry a higher charged price, as real shops charge more for faster shipping.\n- Tune so the default view (2026, USPS commercial, \\$9.99 flat) opens near break-even overall, with losses concentrated where they would be in reality (bulky, far zones).\n- Keep the label: an illustrative home-goods shop, clearly marked as an example (API `description` and the UI).\n\nTasks:\n- [ ] Update `backend/scripts/make_sample_parcels.py` (fixed seed), regenerate `backend/app/sample_parcels.csv`, document the target and the resulting margin in the script docstring.\n- [ ] Regenerate `docs/api-examples/` and the frontend fixtures; update tests that depend on sample-derived numbers.\n- [ ] README: describe the sample in one paragraph.","comments":[],"title":"Make the illustrative sample mix typical and price expedited parcels realistically"}

Add the minimum as data (a column on the oversize rules, with citation) and apply it in billable weight when an oversize rule matches. Found in the PR #17 review.","title":"Model the 90 lb minimum billable weight for large and oversize packages"}
---13---
{"body":"**Why.** The survey in #1 located and downloaded the raw sources through one-off `curl` and WebFetch calls made by agents. Only the re-download step is scripted today (`data/fetch_raw.py` from `data/raw/manifest.csv`). The collection must be reproducible without any LLM, on any machine, so that the data can be refreshed later.\n\n**Goal.** `data/collect.py` (or a small package under `data/collect/`) that rebuilds `data/raw/manifest.csv` and the raw files from scratch, with no agent in the loop.\n\n**Tasks**\n- [ ] Encode each carrier's document patterns in code, not in prose: USPS Notice 123 and price-file zips per price change (`pe.usps.com/Archive/PDF/DMMArchive<date>/Notice123.pdf`, `pe.usps.com/resources/PriceChange/<name>`), USPS press releases, DMM 123/133, UPS Daily Rates guides and surcharge PDFs, FedEx Standard List Rates, surcharge-change PDFs, DAS lists, fuel pages, rate-change pages.\n- [ ] Discovery through the Wayback CDX API where the live site blocks or overwrites files: query by URL prefix, pick the capture by rule (latest 200 capture before a cut-off date), and emit the `id_` passthrough URL. Record the rule so the choice is deterministic.\n- [ ] Discovery of USPS price-change file names via the CDX prefix listing of `pe.usps.com/resources/PriceChange/*`.\n- [ ] Polite by default: sequential, 3 to 5 s pause with jitter, backoff on 429 and 5xx, identifying User-Agent, resumable, never re-fetches a file whose checksum matches.\n- [ ] Output: `manifest.csv` rows (carrier, year, path, url, method, sha256, bytes, in_git, content) and the files, then hand over to `fetch_raw.py` for the download or merge the two scripts.\n- [ ] A `--check` mode that re-runs discovery and reports differences against the committed manifest (new captures, changed checksums) without downloading.\n- [ ] README section \"Data provenance\" explains the full path: `python3 data/collect.py` then `python3 data/fetch_raw.py`, and that no step requires an agent.\n- [ ] Test: discovery functions are unit-tested against recorded CDX responses (fixtures), so the tests run offline.\n\n**Done when** a clean clone on another machine reproduces `data/raw/` and the manifest with the two commands, and the committed manifest matches the result.\n\nRelated: #1, #2.","title":"Script the source discovery so data collection runs end to end without an agent"}
---2---
{"body":"**Goal (~45 min).** Turn the raw sources from #1 into committed, tested CSV tables the backend loads.\n\n**Tables** (under `data/processed/`)\n- `base_rates.csv`: carrier, service, year, zone, weight_lb, price\n- `surcharges.csv`: carrier, year, surcharge, applies_to (service or all), amount, unit (flat or percent), condition (residential, delivery_area, additional_handling, oversize, peak)\n- `fuel_surcharge.csv`: carrier, year, default_percent, note\n- `announced_increase.csv`: carrier, year, percent, source\n- `zone_from_distance.csv`: zone, min_miles, max_miles\n\n**Tasks**\n- [ ] Python parsers under `data/pipeline/` (pdfplumber for PDFs), one module per carrier, runnable with `uv run python -m data.pipeline`.\n- [ ] Golden-sample tests: a handful of hand-checked prices per carrier and year must match the parsed output exactly.\n- [ ] Schema validation of the output tables (no gaps in weight or zone coverage per service and year).\n\n**Done when** the tables are committed, tests pass, and the pipeline is reproducible from the raw files.","title":"Parse raw rate documents into normalized rate tables"}
---3---
{"body":"**Goal (~60 min).** A pure-Python rate engine plus a thin FastAPI layer, with tests.\n\n**Rate engine** (`backend/app/engine/`)\n- [ ] Zone from distance (band table), billable weight = max(actual, dimensional) using each carrier's divisor and rules, rounding up to the next pound.\n- [ ] Quote for one parcel and one service: base rate + each applicable surcharge as a line item (residential, delivery area, additional handling/oversize, peak, fuel percent applied to the right base), total.\n- [ ] Cheapest service per speed tier for a parcel.\n- [ ] Breakeven grid: for a weight × distance grid, which carrier's ground service wins and by how much.\n- [ ] Blended cost for a parcel set per carrier, split into base vs surcharges, and the margin against the flat price charged (or the per-parcel column).\n- [ ] Year-over-year blended cost under each year's prices vs the announced increase, with a breakdown by parcel segment (weight band, distance band, residential, speed) showing which segments drove the change.\n\n**API** (`backend/app/api/`)\n- [ ] `POST /api/quote`, `GET /api/breakeven`, `POST /api/blended`, `POST /api/yearly`, `GET /api/sample-parcels`, `GET /api/meta` (years, services, defaults).\n- [ ] Parcel set input as JSON (the frontend parses CSV) with Pydantic validation and clear 422 errors.\n- [ ] Controls as request fields: year, fuel percent override, peak toggle, flat price, next-year increase percent.\n\n**Tests**\n- [ ] pytest with hand-computed quotes, br

  - Ground Advantage 2023 launch was a decrease (retail −3.2 %, commercial −0.7 %, press 2023-05-10). test_citations' amount check cannot match a negative percent, so no 2023 GA announced_increase row; documented in exceptions.
  - GA nonstandard-fee quotes are short within-line fragments ("in length, add $8.40.", "$18.00.") because the GA footnotes are printed beside the table and page.extract_text() interleaves them with price rows; PM/PME 22–30 in fee quotes are likewise "length, add $4.00." for the same reason. Amounts are correct per the PDF pages cited.
  - Peak/8 % windows use the dates the releases name ("until midnight on Jan. 19, 2025" → effective_to 2025-01-19); the January list starts that same day, so the engine should treat effective_to as exclusive for these rows (stated in notes).
  
  SHARED TESTS / HELPERS
  - test_citations amount check rejects negative percents (see above).
  - SCHEMA says PM/PME steps are 1–70 lb; the documents have a 0.5 lb step for PM commercial and PME; kept and documented.
  - report_changes keys surcharges on exclusive_group; I used a stable "usps_peak" group so year-over-year peak diffs are visible rather than "new rule/removed".
  - Other agents are writing to the same worktree (manifest and data/pipeline/{ups,fedex}* appeared during my run); fetch_raw.py --record rewrites the whole manifest, so concurrent manifest edits are a race.

## Assistant <sub>2026-10-23T08:44:59Z</sub>

The USPS pipeline is done; UPS and FedEx are still running.

**USPS result**
- One command regenerates every USPS table from the CSV zips. It covers 15,516 price cells across 2023 to 2026, both rate cards, zones 1 to 9.
- Every USPS golden cell matches the Notice 123 PDF of the same date, including all the corner cells. The only failure in the full suite is a FedEx test, because the FedEx agent is still working.
- 12 exceptions are documented. One stands out: in 2025, Ground Advantage retail charges less for zone 9 than zone 8 for light parcels. The PDF confirms it, so it's kept as printed.

**Two corrections to my brief**
- **Zone bands.** My guess that UPS zone n equals USPS zone n+1 was wrong. The Domestic Mail Manual shows USPS zones 1 and 2 are separate bands, at 50 and 150 miles. The agent fetched that page through the manifest and the fetcher. It derived UPS and FedEx zone 2 as 0 to 150 miles, with higher zones matching USPS, and wrote that derivation into the table notes.
- **Half-pound step.** Priority Mail commercial and Priority Mail Express have a 0.5 lb step that the schema did not anticipate. It's kept and documented.

**Rule changes found** (feed the year-over-year view)
- Nonstandard fees rose every year.
- The dimensional-weight divisor dropped from 166 to 139 in July 2026, and the dimension rounding changed from "round off" to "round up".
- Peak add-ons are fully transcribed for the 2024, 2025 and 2026 seasons.
- An 8% time-limited increase applies from April 2026.

**Gaps I'll handle at consolidation**
- **Ground Advantage 2023 decrease.** It launched with a price cut, but the citation test can't match a negative percent, so the row is missing. I'll fix the test.
- **Unmodeled fee.** A 2026 "nonstandard characteristic" fee of $4.50 has no condition in the schema. I'll ask you whether to add one.
- **Manifest race.** The fetcher rewrites the whole manifest, so the three agents adding rows concurrently could drop one. I'll verify the manifest row by row once all three finish.

## Assistant <sub>2026-10-23T07:56:11Z</sub>

There is no download script from the survey itself. The three survey agents fetched each file with one-off `curl` commands and WebFetch calls, and recorded the URL and method per file in each carrier's `SOURCES.md`, but they did not save those commands as a script. Their exact commands exist only in their transcripts, which land in `docs/agent-log/` as subagent sections when this session ends.

The one real script is `data/fetch_raw.py`, written afterwards. It is the reproducible path: it re-downloads all 129 files from the URLs and checksums in `data/raw/manifest.csv`, and it has already been proven by restoring the 21 PDFs that were missing.

What is not scripted is the discovery step: finding the right URLs, Wayback timestamps and USPS file names through Wayback CDX index queries and manual browsing. That was interactive research, and I'd describe it as such in the data provenance section rather than pretend otherwise.

If you want the collection to be fully scripted, the clean fix is a `data/discover_sources.py` that queries the CDX index for each known document pattern and emits manifest rows, with the fetcher doing the downloads. That's about an hour I'd rather not spend inside the five-hour budget, so I'd put it in the Extras milestone and document the current method honestly in the README.

Every step of data collection, including discovery of source URLs (e.g. Wayback CDX

touch $M/MEMORY.md && echo "- [Data collection fully scripted](data-collection-fully-scripted.md) — no LLM in the loop; discovery + download must be committed scripts" >> $M/MEMORY.md && cd ~/parcel-rates && gh issue create --title "Script the source discovery so data collection runs end to end without an agent" --milestone "MVP (5h)" --label "data,infra" --body "$(cat <<'EOF'
**Why.** The survey in #1 located and downloaded the raw sources through one-off `curl` and WebFetch calls made by agents. Only the re-download step is scripted today (`data/fetch_raw.py` from `data/raw/manifest.csv`). The collection must be reproducible without any LLM, on any machine, so that the data can be refreshed later.

**Goal.** `data/collect.py` (or a small package under `data/collect/`) that rebuilds `data/raw/manifest.csv` and the raw files from scratch, with no agent in the loop.

**Tasks**
- [ ] Encode each carrier's document patterns in code, not in prose: USPS Notice 123 and price-file zips per price change (`pe.usps.com/Archive/PDF/DMMArchive<date>/Notice123.pdf`, `pe.usps.com/resources/PriceChange/<name>`), USPS press releases, DMM 123/133, UPS Daily Rates guides and surcharge PDFs, FedEx Standard List Rates, surcharge-change PDFs, DAS lists, fuel pages, rate-change pages.
- [ ] Discovery through the Wayback CDX API where the live site blocks or overwrites files: query by URL prefix, pick the capture by rule (latest 200 capture before a cut-off date), and emit the `id_` passthrough URL. Record the rule so the choice is deterministic.
- [ ] Discovery of USPS price-change file names via the CDX prefix listing of `pe.usps.com/resources/PriceChange/*`.
- [ ] Polite by default: sequential, 3 to 5 s pause with jitter, backoff on 429 and 5xx, identifying User-Agent, resumable, never re-fetches a file whose checksum matches.
- [ ] Output: `manifest.csv` rows (carrier, year, path, url, method, sha256, bytes, in_git, content) and the files, then hand over to `fetch_raw.py` for the download or merge the two scripts.
- [ ] A `--check` mode that re-runs discovery and reports differences against the committed manifest (new captures, changed checksums) without downloading.
- [ ] README section "Data provenance" explains the full path: `python3 data/collect.py` then `python3 data/fetch_raw.py`, and that no step requires an agent.
- [ ] Test: discovery functions are unit-tested against recorded CDX responses (fixtures), so the tests run offline.

**Done when** a clean clone on another machine reproduces `data/raw/` and the manifest with the two commands, and the committed manifest matches the result.

Related: #1, #2.

192	## Known limitations
193	
194	- List prices only: a shipper with a negotiated contract pays less.

204	- Peak surcharges are a toggle that prices on December 10 of the year, not by
205	  ship date.
206	- UPS and FedEx bill a large or oversize package at a minimum of 90 lb. That
207	  minimum is not modelled, so the base rate of such parcels is understated;
208	  the surcharge itself is included (issue #18).
209	- Weight is capped at 70 lb, the USPS maximum. Alaska and Hawaii are out of

## 6. package.json

- All new/changed deps are exact-pinned (no `^`/`~`): `@axe-core/playwright: 4.10.2`, `@playwright/test: 1.49.1`, `@types/node: 18.19.130`, matching the installed/cached Playwright version I tested against.
- `overrides.@axe-core/playwright.playwright-core: 1.49.1` correctly pins the transitive playwright-core to match `@playwright/test`, avoiding a dual-Playwright-version install.
- `vite.config.ts` test.include is now `["src/**/*.test.{ts,tsx}"]`, confirmed via the 195-test vitest run that no `e2e/*.spec.ts` files were collected.
- `"e2e": "playwright test --grep-invert @screens"` / `"e2e:update-screens": "playwright test --grep @screens"` cleanly separates the deterministic functional/a11y suite from the screenshot-regeneration path.

## Bottom line

Work ONLY inside ~/parcel-rates-issue-2 (a git worktree on branch issue-2-rate-tables). Do not commit. Do not read other directories on this machine. Use absolute paths in every shell command (the shell's working directory resets between calls).

Goal: build `data/raw/manifest.csv` listing every raw source file, then use `data/fetch_raw.py` to restore the PDFs that are no longer on disk and record their checksums. Read `docs/data/SCHEMA.md` section "Raw manifest" for the columns: carrier, year, path, url, method, sha256, bytes, in_git, content.

Context:
- `data/raw/usps/SOURCES.md`, `data/raw/ups/SOURCES.md`, `data/raw/fedex/SOURCES.md` list every file that was downloaded during the survey, with its URL and retrieval method. Some files were fetched through the Wayback Machine; for those, the manifest `url` must be the passthrough form `https://web.archive.org/web/<timestamp>id_/<original-url>` (note the `id_` suffix after the timestamp) so the original bytes are returned. Use the Wayback timestamp given in SOURCES.md; if only the original URL is given and the method says Wayback, look up a usable timestamp via the CDX API (`https://web.archive.org/cdx/search/cdx?url=<url>&output=json&limit=5&filter=statuscode:200`).
- Files currently on disk under `data/raw/` are the ones tracked in git (spreadsheets, CSV zips, HTML, press-release and list-rate PDFs) plus `data/raw/usps/dmm/*.htm` (four DMM pages I fetched: dmm123 and dmm133, each as a 2024-03 Wayback capture and a 2026-10-22 live capture from https://pe.usps.com/text/dmm300/123.htm and /133.htm; the 2024 ones came from https://web.archive.org/web/20240304155059id_/https://pe.usps.com/text/dmm300/123.htm and https://web.archive.org/web/20240225004436id_/https://pe.usps.com/text/dmm300/133.htm and were gunzipped after download).
- The large "full guide" PDFs listed in SOURCES.md (UPS yearly Daily Rates guides, FedEx Service Guides, most USPS Notice 123 PDFs) are NOT on disk any more. They must appear in the manifest with in_git=false and get re-fetched by the script.
- `in_git` is `true` for .xlsx, .zip, .csv, .htm/.html files and `false` for every .pdf (PDFs are gitignored).
- `content` is one short line describing the file (take it from SOURCES.md).

Steps:
1. Write the manifest with every file from the three SOURCES.md files plus the four DMM pages. Rows for files present on disk get sha256 (`sha256sum`) and bytes now. Rows for absent files leave sha256 and bytes empty.
2. Run `python3 ~/parcel-rates-issue-2/data/fetch_raw.py --record` ONCE, in the foreground, and let it finish (it is sequential and pauses between requests on purpose; expect 10 to 25 minutes for ~30 large PDFs; set a long timeout). Do not run it in parallel, do not write your own downloader, do not fetch with curl yourself: the whole point is to be polite to pe.usps.com and web.archive.org. If the script reports failures for some rows, fix their URLs (wrong timestamp, missing id_, etc.) and re-run the script once more; it skips files whose checksum already matches.
3. Run `python3 ~/parcel-rates-issue-2/data/fetch_raw.py --verify` and make sure it reports no problems.
4. Spot-check that re-fetched PDFs are real PDFs (`file` or `head -c 5` shows `%PDF`), and that the FedEx Standard List Rates 2026 Wayback capture dated 2026-10-22 and the UPS 2026 guide are among them.

Report back: the number of manifest rows, how many were fetched, any rows that could not be restored (with the URL tried and the error), total bytes on disk under data/raw, and anything in SOURCES.md you found inconsistent (do not edit SOURCES.md).
